Theorem 4.13.
 \Psi  is the eigenvector of  X  with the eigenvalue  [N+M] .
The multiplicity is one.
Proof.
From Theorem 4.10, the multiplicity of the eigenvalue  [N+M] 
is obviously one.
Let  (X_{D,D^{\prime}})  be the matrix representation of  X .
We will show that
(28)
 \displaystyle\sum_{D^{\prime}}X_{D,D^{\prime}}\Psi_{D^{\prime}}=[N+M]\Psi_{D}. 
We have four cases for  D :
1)  D  does not have down arrows,
2)  2\leq r\leq M ,
3)  N_{1}=1  and
4)  r=1  and  N_{1}=0 .
Case 1
Let  D  be a diagram starting with  n_{1}  up arrows, followed by
an outer arc of size  m_{1} , followed by  n_{2}  up arrows, followed
by an outer arc of size  m_{2} ,  \cdots  and ending with  n_{I+1} 
up arrows. Inside of an outer arc of size  m_{i} ,  1\leq i\leq I , is
filled with arcs.
Set  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and  N_{\uparrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer arc
of size  m_{i}  to two up arrows.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}] .
The contribution of these  D^{\prime} ’s to the left hand side
of Eqn.(28) is
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{I}\left[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}\right][m_{i}]\frac{\prod_{j\geq i+1}[1+\sum_{k=1}^{j}(n_{k}+m_{k})]}{\prod_{j\geq i}[1+n_{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}(n_{k}+m_{k})]}(q^{N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+M}+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+M)})\Psi_{D}. 
Inserting Lemma A.2,
we obtain  [M^{\prime}](q^{N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+M}+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+M)})\Psi_{D} .
From Eqn.(25), the contribution of the diagonal term
is  [N_{\uparrow}+M]\Psi_{D} .
Note  N=N_{\uparrow}+2M^{\prime} .
The sum of these two contributions is the right hand side of
Eqn.(28).
Case 2
In this case, we have  S_{R}=S_{W}=S_{L}=\emptyset .
Let  D  be a diagram starting with  n_{1}  up arrows, followed by
an outer arc of size  m_{1} , followed by  n_{2}  up arrows, followed
by an outer arc of size  m_{2} ,  \cdots , followed by  n_{I+1}  up
arrows, followed by a down arrow with the integer  r  and ending
with  G  arcs,  |T|  dashed arcs and down arrows with the
integer  p ,  r+1\leq p\leq M .
Set  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and  N_{\uparrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer arc
of size  m_{i}  to two up arrows.
By a similar argument to Case 1, the contribution of these  D^{\prime} ’s to
the left hand side of Eqn.(28) is
 \displaystyle\frac{[M^{\prime}][1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+G+M-r+1]}{[1+M^{\prime}+N_{\uparrow}]}(q^{N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+G+M}+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+G+M)})\Psi_{D}. 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained form  D  by changing the down arrow
with the integer  r  to an up arrow.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[N_{\uparrow}+1] .
The contribution of this diagram is
 \displaystyle[N_{\uparrow}+1]\frac{[G+M-r+1]}{[1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}]}(q^{N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+G+M}+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+G+M)})\Psi_{D}. 
The contribution of the diagonal term is  [N_{\uparrow}+r-1]\Psi_{D} .
Note that  N=N_{\uparrow}+2M^{\prime}+2G+M-r+1 .
The sum of these three contributions is the right hand side of
Eqn.(28).
Case 3
Let  D  be diagram starting with  n_{1}  up arrows, followed by
an outer arc of size  m_{1} , followed by  n_{2}  up arrows, followed
by an outer arc of size  m_{2} ,  \cdots , followed by  n_{I+1}  up
arrows, followed by an unpaired down arrow and ending with
 |S_{W}|+|S_{W^{\prime}}|+|S_{R}|  arcs,  |T|  dashed arcs, a down
arrow with a star and down arrows with an integer  p ,  2\leq p\leq M .
Set  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and  N_{\uparrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer arc
of size  m_{i}  to two up arrows.
By a similar argument to Case 1, the contribution of these  D^{\prime} ’s
to the left hand side of (28) is
 \displaystyle[M^{\prime}]\frac{[1+d]}{[1+M^{\prime}+N_{\uparrow}]}(q^{d+1}+q^{-(d+1)}), 
where  d=N_{\uparrow}+|S|+|T|+M .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the unpaired
down arrow to an up arrow.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[N_{\uparrow}+1] .
The contribution is
 \displaystyle[N_{\uparrow}+1]\frac{[1+N_{\uparrow}+2|S|-M^{\prime}+2|T|+2M]}{[1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}]}. 
Note that  N=N_{\uparrow}+2|S|+2|T|+M+1 .
The sum of two contributions gives the right hand side of Eqn.(28).
Case 4
Let  D  be diagram starting with  n_{1}  up arrows, followed by
an outer arc of size  m_{1} , followed by  n_{2}  up arrows, followed
by an outer arc of size  m_{2} ,  \cdots , followed by  n_{I+1}  up
arrows and ending with
 |S_{W}|+|S_{W^{\prime}}|+|S_{R}|  arcs,  |T|  dashed arcs, a down
arrow with a star and down arrows with an integer  p ,  2\leq p\leq M .
We have two cases for  D : a)  n_{I+1}\neq 0 , that is,  |S_{L}|=\emptyset 
and b) for a given  H  satisfying  1\leq H\leq I ,  n_{H+1}\neq 0  and
 n_{i}=0  for  H+2\leq i\leq I+1 .
Set  N_{\uparrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i} ,  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i} 
and  d=N_{\uparrow}+|S|+|T|+M .
Case 4-a
Set  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and  L_{1}:=N_{\uparrow}+2|S|-M^{\prime}+2|T|+2M .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained form  D  by changing the outer arc of
size  m_{i} ,  1\leq i\leq I  to two up arrows.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}] .
By a similar argument to Case 1, the contribution to the left hand side of
(28) is
(29)
 \displaystyle[M^{\prime}]\frac{[d][L_{1}+1]}{[N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+1][L_{1}]}(q^{d}+q^{-d})\Psi_{D}. 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the leftmost dashed
arc to an up arrow and an unpaired down arrow.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[N_{\uparrow}+1] .
The contribution is
(30)
 \displaystyle[N_{\uparrow}+1]\frac{[|S_{W^{\prime}}|+|S_{W}|+|T|+|S_{R}|+M][d]}{[L_{1}][1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}]}(q^{d}+q^{-d})(q^{d^{\prime}}+q^{-d^{\prime}})\Psi_{D}, 
where  d^{\prime}=d-N_{\uparrow}-M^{\prime} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the rightmost up arrow to
an unpaired down arrow. We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[N_{\uparrow}] .
The contribution is
(31)
 \displaystyle[N_{\uparrow}]\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}]}{[L_{1}]}\Psi_{D}. 
Note  N=N_{\uparrow}+2|S|+2|T|+M .
The sum of three contributions (29), (30) and (31)
gives the right hand side of Eqn.(28).
Case 4-b
We have  S_{L}\neq\emptyset  and  |S_{L}|=\sum_{i=H+1}^{I}m_{i} .
Set  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{H}m_{i}  and  L_{1}:=N_{\uparrow}+2|S|-M^{\prime}+2|T|+2M .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer arc of size
 m_{i} ,  1\leq i\leq H , to two up arrows.
By a similar argument to Case 1, the contribution of these  D^{\prime} ’s to the
left hand side of (28) is
(32)
 \displaystyle[M^{\prime}]\frac{[L_{1}+1]}{[L_{1}]}\frac{[d]}{[1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}]}(q^{d}+q^{-d})\Psi_{D}. 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer arc of
size  m_{i} ,  H+1\leq i\leq I , to two up arrows.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[N_{\uparrow}+1] .
Thus, the contribution of these  D^{\prime} ’s is
(33)
 \displaystyle\Psi_{D}\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+1][d]}{[1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+|S_{L}|][L_{1}]}(q^{d}+q^{-d})\sum_{i=H+1}^{I}[m_{i}][l_{i}]\frac{\prod_{j\geq i+1}[w_{j}]}{\prod_{j\geq i}[w_{j-1}]}\prod_{j=H+1}^{i}\frac{[g_{j}]}{[g_{j}-m_{j}]} 
where
 \displaystyle l_{i} 
 \displaystyle:= 
 \displaystyle 1+N_{\uparrow}+2|S|-M^{\prime}+2|T|+2M-\sum_{j=H+1}^{i}m_{j}, 
 \displaystyle w_{j} 
 \displaystyle:= 
 \displaystyle 1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+\sum_{k=H+1}^{j}m_{k}, 
 \displaystyle g_{i} 
 \displaystyle:= 
 \displaystyle 2|S_{R}|+2|S_{W^{\prime}}|+2|S_{W}|+2M+2|T|+2\sum_{j=i}^{I}m_{j}. 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the leftmost
dashed arc to an up arrow and a down arrow.
The contribution of  D^{\prime}  is
(34)
 \displaystyle\Psi_{D}[N_{\uparrow}+1]\frac{[2L_{2}][N_{\uparrow}+|S|+|T|+M]}{[L_{1}][1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}+|S_{L}|]}(q^{d}+q^{-d})\prod_{i=H+1}^{I}\frac{[g_{i}]}{[g_{i}-m_{i}]}, 
where  L_{2}:=|S_{W^{\prime}}|+|S_{W}|+|T|+|S_{R}|+M .
By Lemma A.9 with  x=N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}  and
 z=|S_{W^{\prime}}|+|S_{W}|+|S_{R}|+|T|+M-1 ,
the sum of contributions (33) and
(34) is given by
(35)
 \displaystyle\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+1][N_{\uparrow}+|S|+|T|+M][2L_{2}+2|S_{L}|]}{[L_{1}][1+N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}]}(q^{d}+q^{-d})\Psi_{D}. 
Finally, let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing
the rightmost up arrow to an unpaired down arrow.
The contribution of this  D^{\prime}  is
(36)
 \displaystyle\frac{[N_{\uparrow}][N_{\uparrow}+M^{\prime}]}{[L_{1}]}\Psi_{D}. 
The sum of the contributions (32), (35) and
(36) gives the right hand side of Eqn.(28).
This completes the proof.
∎
4.3. Type BII
We consider the action of  X  on the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of type BII.
Let  D  be a diagram of type BII and  N_{\uparrow}  be the number of
(unpaired) up arrows.
we enumerate the up arrows from left to right by  1,2,\ldots,N_{\uparrow} .
For each  i ,  1\leq i<N_{\uparrow} , we denote by  X_{(i)}(D)  a diagram
obtained from  D  by connecting the  i -th up arrow and  (i+1) -th up arrow
via an arc.
We denote by  X_{(N_{\uparrow})}(D)  a diagram obtained from  D  by changing
the  N_{\uparrow} -th up arrow to an e-unpaired or an o-unpaired down arrow.
Suppose that  D  does not have down arrows or the leftmost down arrow
of  D  is an e-unpaired down arrow.
We define the action of  X  by
(37)
 \displaystyle X(D):=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\uparrow}}[i]X_{(i)}(D)+[Q;N_{\uparrow}]D. 
Suppose that the leftmost down arrow of  D  is an o-unpaired down arrow.
Then, we define the action of  X  by
(38)
 \displaystyle X(D):=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\uparrow}}[i]X_{(i)}(D)+[Q;-N_{\uparrow}-1]D.

The action of  X  on  D  is
 \displaystyle X(D)=X_{(1)}(D)+[2]X_{(2)}(D)+[Q;2]D. 
Theorem 4.15.
The action of  X  defined in Eqns.(37) and (38)
provides the action on the Kazhdan–Lusztig bases of type BII.
Proof.
We prove Theorem by induction.
When  N=1 , Theorem is true by a straightforward calculation.
We assume that Theorem holds true for diagrams
of length up to  N-1 .
Let  D  be a diagram of length  N .
We have two cases for the leftmost arrow  a  of  D :
1) the arrow  a  is an up arrow and
2) the arrow  a  is a down arrow.
Case 1
A diagram  D  is written as  \uparrow D^{\prime}  where  D^{\prime}  is a diagram of length  N-1 .
The action of  X  on  D  is
(39)
 \displaystyle X(\uparrow D^{\prime})=q\uparrow X(D^{\prime})+\downarrow D^{\prime}. 
We have two cases for the leftmost down arrow  a^{\prime}  of  D^{\prime} :
a) the arrow  a^{\prime}  is an e-unpaired down arrow and
b) the arrow  a^{\prime}  is an o-unpaired down arrow.
Case 1-a
From Eqn.(37) for  D^{\prime} , we have
(40)
 \displaystyle q\uparrow X(D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{N_{\uparrow}}q[i-1]X_{(i)}(D)+q[Q;N_{\uparrow}-1]D, 
(41)
 \displaystyle\downarrow D^{\prime} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\uparrow}}q^{-(i-1)}X_{(i)}(D)+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}-1)}Q^{-1}D. 
From  q[i-1]+q^{-(i-1)}=[i]  and
 q[Q;N_{\uparrow}-1]+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}-1)}Q^{-1}=[Q;N_{\uparrow}] ,
the sum of Eqns.(40) and (41) is equal to the
right hand side of Eqn.(37).
Case 1-b
Similarly, we have
 \displaystyle q\uparrow X(D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{N_{\uparrow}}q[i-1]X_{(i)}(D)+q[Q;-N_{\uparrow}]D, 
 \displaystyle\downarrow D^{\prime} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\uparrow}}q^{-(i-1)}X_{(i)}(D)-q^{-N_{\uparrow}}QD 
From  q[Q;-N_{\uparrow}]-q^{-N_{\uparrow}}Q=[Q;-N_{\uparrow}-1] ,
the sum of  q\uparrow X(D^{\prime})  and  \downarrow D^{\prime}  is equal to the
right hand side of Eqn.(38).
Case 2
We have three cases for the leftmost down arrow  a  of  D :
a) the arrow  a  is an e-unpaired down arrow,
b) the arrow  a  is an o-unpaired down arrow and
c) the arrow  a  forms an arc.
Case 2-a
The diagram  D  is written as
 \raisebox{-0.75pt}{
\hbox to11.11pt{\vbox to25.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.55522pt\lower-25.40262pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 -19.69 M 0 -27.56}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.22221pt}{-22.06961pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.07 -30.54)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\!\!\!D^{\prime} 
where  D^{\prime}  is a diagram of length  N-1 .
We have
 \displaystyle X(D) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle X(\downarrow D^{\prime})+q^{-1}QX(\uparrow D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle q^{-1}\downarrow X(D^{\prime})+\uparrow D^{\prime}+Q\uparrow X(D^{\prime})+q^{-1}Q\downarrow D^{\prime} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle[Q;0]D 
where we have used  X(D^{\prime})=[Q;-1]D^{\prime} .
Case 2-b
The diagram  D  is written as
 \raisebox{-0.75pt}{
\hbox to11.67pt{\vbox to25.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.83301pt\lower-25.40262pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 -19.69 M 0 -27.56}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-22.06961pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.46 -30.54)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}\!\!\!D^{\prime} 
where  D^{\prime}  is a diagram of length  N-1 .
We have
 \displaystyle X(D) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle X(\downarrow D^{\prime})-Q^{-1}X(\uparrow D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle q^{-1}\downarrow X(D^{\prime})+\uparrow D^{\prime}-qQ^{-1}\uparrow X(D^{\prime})-Q^{-1}\downarrow D^{\prime} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle[Q;-1]D 
where we have used  X(D^{\prime})=[Q;0]D^{\prime} .
Case 2-c
By a similar argument to Case 2-b in the proof of Theorem 4.9,
we can assume  D  is written as
 D=\raisebox{-0.5pt}{
\hbox to17.47pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -23.62 23.62 -23.62 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}D^{\prime}  where  D^{\prime}  is a diagram of length  N-2 .
We have
 \displaystyle X(D) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle X(\downarrow\uparrow D^{\prime})-q^{-1}X(\uparrow\downarrow D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle q^{-1}\downarrow X(\uparrow D^{\prime})+\uparrow\uparrow D^{\prime}-\uparrow X(\downarrow D^{\prime})-q^{-1}\downarrow\downarrow D^{\prime} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\downarrow\uparrow X(D^{\prime})+q^{-1}\downarrow\downarrow+\uparrow\uparrow D^{\prime}-q^{-1}\uparrow\downarrow X(D^{\prime})-\uparrow\uparrow D^{\prime}-q^{-1}\downarrow\downarrow D^{\prime} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\raisebox{-0.5pt}{
\hbox to17.47pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -23.62 23.62 -23.62 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}X(D^{\prime}). 
In both Case 1 and 2,  X(D)  coincides with the definitions (37)
and (38). This completes the proof.
∎
Theorem 4.16.
 X  has the eigenvalue  [Q;N-2i] ,  0\leq i\leq N ,
of multiplicity  \displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{i} .
Proof.
Recall that a diagram  D  is constructed from a binary string
 b .
The lexicographic order of binary strings induces a natural
lexicographic order of diagrams.
We consider the matrix representation of  X  on
the Kazhdan–Lusztig bases.
In the lexicographic order of bases,  X  is a lower triangular
matrix.
From Eqns.(37) and (38),
the diagonal entries of  X  are
 [Q;N_{\uparrow}]  or  [Q;-N_{\uparrow}-1] .
Thus an eigenvalue of  X  is of the form  [Q;n]  with some  n .
The multiplicity of an eigenvalue  [Q;n] ,  n\in\mathbb{N} ,
is the number of diagrams which has  n  up arrows and the leftmost
down arrow is e-unpaired down arrow.
Similarly, the multiplicity of an eigenvalue  [Q;-n-1] ,  n\in\mathbb{N} ,
is the number of diagrams which has  n  up arrows and the leftmost
down arrow is o-unpaired down arrow.
Set  \lambda_{D}=n  (resp.  \lambda_{D}=-n-1 ) if the leftmost down arrow
is e-unpaired (resp. o-unpaired) down arrow.
If  D  does not have down arrows, we set  \lambda_{D}=n .
Suppose that a diagram  D  has  m  arcs.
We denote by  D^{\prime}  a diagram obtained from  D  by removing all the arcs.
The diagram  D^{\prime}  is of length  N-2m  and without arcs.
We denote by  \mathcal{D^{\prime}}  the set of such  D^{\prime} ’s. The cardinality of
 \mathcal{D^{\prime}}  is  N-2m+1 .
Reversely, if we have a diagram  D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D^{\prime}} , one can construct
a diagram  D  by inserting arcs into  D^{\prime} .
Given  D^{\prime} , the number of possible  D ’s is given
by  \displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{m}-\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{m-1} .
Obviously, we have  \lambda_{D^{\prime}}=\lambda_{D} .
Given a diagram  D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D^{\prime}} , we have  \lambda_{D^{\prime}}=N-2m-2j  with some
 j ,  0\leq j\leq N-2m . Note that there is a one-to-one correspondence between
 j  and  D^{\prime} .
Thus the number of diagrams for  \lambda_{D}=N-2i ,  0\leq i\leq N ,
is
 \displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\min(i,N-i)}\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{k}-\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{k-1} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{\min(i,N-i)} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{i} 
This completes the proof.
∎
Let  D  be a diagram of Type BII.
We denote by  S  the set of arcs, by  S_{\uparrow} 
the set of up arrows, by  S_{\mathrm{e}}  the
set of e-unpaired down arrows and by  S_{\mathrm{o}} 
the set of o-unpaired down arrows.
We enumerate arcs, up arrows and e-unpaired down arrows
from left.
Let  N_{A}  be the integer assigned to
 A\in S\cup S_{\uparrow}\cup S_{\mathrm{e}} .
We define
 \displaystyle N_{1}: 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\prod_{A\in S\cup S_{\mathrm{e}}}[N_{A}], 
 \displaystyle N_{2}: 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\prod_{B\in S}[m_{B}]^{-1}. 
where  m_{B}  is the size of  B\in S .
We enumerate arcs and e-unpaired from right to left by  1,2,\ldots .
If there exist arcs inside of an arc or a dashed arc, we increase the
integer one by one from inside to outside.
Let  N_{C}  be the integer assigned to
 C\in S\cup S_{\mathrm{e}} .
We define
 \displaystyle N_{3}:=\prod_{C\in S_{\mathrm{e}}}[N_{C}]^{-1}. 
We enumerate arcs, up arrows and o-unpaired down arrows
from right.
Let  N_{E}  be the integer assigned to
 E\in S\cup S_{\uparrow}\cup S_{\mathrm{o}} .
We define
 \displaystyle N_{4}:=\prod_{E\in S\cup S_{\uparrow}}(q^{N_{E}-1}Q+q^{-(N_{E}-1)}Q^{-1}). 
In the above notation, we define
Definition 4.17.
 \Psi_{D}=N_{1}\cdot N_{2}\cdot N_{3}\cdot N_{4} .

}. 
We have
 \displaystyle N_{1}=\frac{[6]!}{[2]},\qquad N_{2}=[2]^{-1},\qquad N_{3}=[3]^{-1},\qquad N_{4}=\prod_{i=2}^{6}(q^{i-1}Q+q^{-(i-1)}Q^{-1}). 
Theorem 4.19.
 \Psi  is the eigenvector of  X  with the eigenvalue  [Q;N] .
The multiplicity is one.
Proof.
Let  X=(X_{D,D^{\prime}})  be the matrix representation of  X .
We show that
(42)
 \displaystyle\sum_{D^{\prime}}X_{D,D^{\prime}}\Psi_{D^{\prime}}=[Q;N]\Psi_{D}. 
We have three cases for  D : 1)  D  does not have down arrows,
2) the leftmost down arrow of  D  is an e-unpaired down arrow
and 3) the leftmost down arrow of  D  is an o-unpaired down arrow.
Case 1
Let  D  be a diagram starting with  n_{1}  up arrows, followed by
an outer arc of size  m_{1} , followed by  n_{2}  up arrows, followed
by an outer arc of size  m_{2} ,  \cdots  and ending with  n_{I+1} 
up arrows. Inside of an outer arc of size  m_{i} ,  1\leq i\leq I , is
filled with arcs.
As a diagram,  D  is
(43)
 \displaystyle\underbrace{\uparrow\ldots\uparrow}_{n_{1}}\!\!\raisebox{-0.8pt}{
\hbox to38.88pt{\vbox to30.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.21198pt\lower-30.18459pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -31.5 39.37 -31.5 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.87897pt}{-25.35158pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.6 -35.08)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\!\!\uparrow\ldots\uparrow\!\!\!\raisebox{-0.8pt}{
\hbox to38.98pt{\vbox to30.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.26302pt\lower-30.18459pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -31.5 39.37 -31.5 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 -31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.93001pt}{-25.35158pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.67 -35.08)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\!\!\underbrace{\uparrow\ldots\uparrow}_{n_{I+1}}.  m_{1}  m_{I} 
Set  N_{\uparrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i}  and  M=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i} .
The component  \Psi_{D}  is explicitly written as
 \displaystyle\Psi_{D}=\prod_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[\sum_{j=1}^{i}(n_{j}+m_{j})]}{[n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{j}+m_{j})]}\cdot\prod_{B\in S}[m_{B}]^{-1}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N_{\uparrow}+M}(Qq^{i-1}+Q^{-1}q^{-(i-1)}). 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the arc of
size  m_{i}  to two up arrows. Then, we have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}] .
The contribution to the left hand side of Eqn.(42) is
 \displaystyle\Psi_{D}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\left[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}\right][m_{i}]\frac{\prod_{j\geq i+1}^{I}[1+\sum_{k=1}^{j}(n_{k}+m_{k})]}{\prod_{j\geq i}^{I}[1+n_{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}(n_{k}+m_{k})]}(q^{N_{\uparrow}+M}Q+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}+M)}Q^{-1}). 
Inserting Lemma A.2, the above expression is reduced to
 \displaystyle[M](q^{N_{\uparrow}+M}Q+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}+M)}Q^{-1})\Psi_{D}. 
The contribution of the diagonal term is  [Q;N_{\uparrow}]\Psi_{D} .
Therefore, the left hand side of Eqn.(42) is
 \displaystyle[M](q^{N_{\uparrow}+M}Q+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}+M)}Q^{-1})\Psi_{D}+[Q;N_{\uparrow}]\Psi_{D} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle[Q;N_{\uparrow}+2M]\Psi_{D} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle[Q;N]\Psi_{D}. 
Case 2
Let  D  be a diagram starting with  n_{1}  up arrows, followed by
an outer arc of size  m_{1} , followed by  n_{2}  up arrows, followed
by an outer arc of size  m_{2} ,  \cdots , followed by  n_{I+1} 
up arrows, followed by e-unpaired down arrow and ending with
 G-1  e-unpaired down arrows,  M^{\prime}  arcs and  G  o-unpaired down
arrows.
Set  N_{\uparrow}:=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i}  and  M=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer arc
of size  m_{i}  to two up arrows.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}] .
By a similar calculation to Case 1, the contribution to the left
hand side of Eqn.(42) is
 \displaystyle\frac{[M][d+1]}{[1+M+N_{\uparrow}]}(Qq^{d}+Q^{-1}q^{-d})\Psi_{D} 
where  d=G+M+M^{\prime}+N .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the leftmost
down arrow to an up arrow.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[N_{\uparrow}+1] .
The contribution to the left hand side of Eqn.(42) is
(44)
 \displaystyle\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+1][G+M^{\prime}]}{[1+M+N_{\uparrow}]}(Qq^{d}+Q^{-1}q^{-d})\Psi_{D}. 
The contribution of the diagonal term is  [Q;N]\Psi_{D} .
Note that  N=N_{\uparrow}+2M+2M^{\prime}+2G .
The sum of three contributions gives the right hand side of (42).
Case 3
Let  D  be a diagram starting with  n_{1}  up arrows, followed by
an outer arc of size  m_{1} , followed by  n_{2}  up arrows, followed
by an outer arc of size  m_{2} ,  \cdots , followed by  n_{I+1} 
up arrows, followed by o-unpaired down arrow and ending with
 G-1  e-unpaired down arrows,  M^{\prime}  arcs and  G-1  o-unpaired
down arrows.
We set  N_{\uparrow}:=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i}  and
 M:=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the arc of
size  m_{i}  to two up arrows.
By a similar calculation to Case 2, the contribution to the left
hand side of (42) is
 \displaystyle\frac{[M][d]}{[1+M+N_{\uparrow}]}(Qq^{d}+Q^{-1}q^{-d})\Psi_{D}. 
where  d=M+M^{\prime}+N_{\uparrow}+G .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the leftmost
down arrow to an up arrow. The contribution of this diagram is
 \displaystyle\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+1][d]}{[1+M+N_{\uparrow}]}(Qq^{M^{\prime}+G-1}+Q^{-1}q^{-(M^{\prime}+G-1)})\Psi_{D}. 
The contribution of the diagonal term is  [Q;-N_{\uparrow}-1]\Psi_{D} .
Note that  N=N_{\uparrow}+2M+2M^{\prime}+2G-1 .
The sum of three contributions gives the right hand side of Eqn.(42).
This completes the proof.
∎
4.4. Type BIII
We consider the action of  X  on Kazhdan–Lusztig bases of type BIII.
Let  D  be a diagram of type BIII,  N_{\uparrow}  be the number
of up arrows and  N_{\downarrow}  be the number of down arrows
with a circled integer.
We define the weight of  D  by  \mathrm{wt}(D):=N_{\uparrow}-N_{\downarrow} .
We enumerate the up arrows from left to right by
 1,2,\ldots,N_{\uparrow} .
For each  1\leq i<N_{\uparrow} , we denote by  X_{(i)}(D)  a diagram
obtained from  D  by connecting the  i -th up arrow and the  (i+1) -th
up arrow via an arc.
We denote by  X_{(N_{\uparrow})}(D)  a diagram obtained from  D  by
changing the  N_{\uparrow} -th up arrow to the down arrow with a
circled integer  N_{\downarrow}+1 .
We define the action of  X  by
(45)
 \displaystyle X(D):=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\uparrow}}[i]X_{(i)}(D)+[Q;\mathrm{wt}(D)]D. 
Example 4.20.
Let  D  be a diagram depicted as
 \displaystyle D=\raisebox{-0.6pt}{
\hbox to138.31pt{\vbox to28.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.61418pt\lower-27.71483pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} 
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}{{}}{}
{}{}

Then, the action of  X  on  D  is
 \displaystyle X(D)=X_{(1)}(D)+[2]X_{(2)}(D)+[Q;0]D. 
Theorem 4.21.
The action of  X  defined in Eqn.(45) provides
the action of  X  on the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis of Type BIII.
Proof.
We prove Theorem by induction.
When  N=1 , Theorem is true by a straightforward calculation.
We assume that Theorem holds true for diagrams of length up to  N-1 .
Let  D  be a diagram of length  N .
We have two cases for the leftmost arrow  a  of  D :
1) the arrow  a  is an up arrow and
2) the arrow  a  is a down arrow.
Case 1
A diagram  D  is written as  \uparrow D^{\prime}  where  D^{\prime}  is a diagram of
length  N-1 .
The action of  X  on  D  is
(46)
 \displaystyle X(\uparrow D^{\prime})=q\uparrow X(D^{\prime})+\downarrow D. 
From the assumption, we have
 \displaystyle\uparrow X(D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{N_{\uparrow}}[i-1]t_{(i)}(D)+[Q;\mathrm{wt}(D)-1], 
 \displaystyle\downarrow D^{\prime} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\uparrow}}q^{-(i-1)}Q^{-1}X_{(i)}(D)+q^{-N_{\uparrow}+N_{\downarrow}+1}. 
Note that  q[Q;d]+q^{-d}Q^{-1}=[Q;d+1] .
Inserting these two expressions into Eqn.(46),
we obtain Eqn.(45).
Case 2
We have two cases for the leftmost arrow  a :
i) the arrow  a  is a down arrow with a circled integer  r  and
ii) the arrow  a  forms an arc.
Case 2-i
A diagram  D  is written as
 \raisebox{-0.6pt}{
\hbox to9.64pt{\vbox to18.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.82231pt\lower-18.18053pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 -11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.4 -18.48 C 6.4 -14.95 3.53 -12.09 0 -12.09 C -3.53 -12.09 -6.4 -14.95 -6.4 -18.48 C -6.4 -22.02 -3.53 -24.88 0 -24.88 C 3.53 -24.88 6.4 -22.02 6.4 -18.48 Z M 0 -18.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.39468pt}{-15.51099pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.31 -21.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}D^{\prime} 
where  D^{\prime}  is a diagram of length  N-1 . The weight of  D  is  -r .
We have
 \displaystyle X(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{
\hbox to9.64pt{\vbox to18.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.82231pt\lower-18.18053pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 -11.81}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.4 -18.48 C 6.4 -14.95 3.53 -12.09 0 -12.09 C -3.53 -12.09 -6.4 -14.95 -6.4 -18.48 C -6.4 -22.02 -3.53 -24.88 0 -24.88 C 3.53 -24.88 6.4 -22.02 6.4 -18.48 Z M 0 -18.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.39468pt}{-15.51099pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.31 -21.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle q^{-1}\downarrow X(D^{\prime})+\uparrow D^{\prime}-q^{r}Q^{-1}\uparrow X(D^{\prime})-q^{r-1}Q^{-1}\downarrow D^{\prime}, 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle[Q;-r]D 
where we have used  X(D^{\prime})=[Q;-(r-1)]D^{\prime} ,  q^{-1}[Q;-(r-1)]-q^{r-1}Q^{-1}=[Q;-r] 
and  1-q^{r}Q^{-1}[Q;-(r-1)]=-q^{r-1}Q^{-1}[Q;-r] .
Case 2-ii
By a similar argument to Case 2-b in the proof of Theorem 4.9,
we can assume that a diagram  D  is written as
 \raisebox{-0.5pt}{
\hbox to17.47pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -23.62 23.62 -23.62 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}D^{\prime}  where  D^{\prime}  is a diagram of length  N-2 .
We have
 \displaystyle X(\raisebox{-0.5pt}{
\hbox to17.47pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -23.62 23.62 -23.62 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle X(\downarrow\uparrow D^{\prime}-q^{-1}\uparrow\downarrow D^{\prime}) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\raisebox{-0.5pt}{
\hbox to17.47pt{\vbox to17.47pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-17.27182pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -23.62 23.62 -23.62 23.62 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}X(D^{\prime}). 
In both Case 1 and 2, we have Eqn.(45) for
a diagram  D .
This completes the proof.
∎
Theorem 4.22.
 X  has the eigenvalue  [Q;N-2i] , 1\leq i\leq N , of multiplicity
 \displaystyle\genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{i} .
Proof.
Let  X_{D,D^{\prime}}  be a matrix representation of  X  with respect to
diagrams.
Recall that there is a one-to-one correspondence between
a binary string and a diagram.
Thus we have a natural order for diagrams induced from
the lexicographic order for binary strings.
From Eqn.(45), the matrix  X_{D,D^{\prime}}  is
a lower triangular matrix.
Since the diagonal terms are  [Q;\mathrm{wt}(D)] , the
eigenvalues are of the form  [Q;N-2i] .
The multiplicities are equal to the number of  D  such
that  \mathrm{wt}(D)=N-2j .
By a similar argument to the second paragraph in the
proof of Theorem 4.16, the cardinality
of  D  satisfying  \mathrm{wt}(D)=N-2j  is
 \genfrac{(}{)}{0.0pt}{}{N}{j} .
∎
Let  D  be a diagram of type BIII,  S  be the set of arcs,
 S_{\uparrow}  be the set of (unpaired) up arrows and
 S_{\downarrow}  be the set of down arrows with a circled integer.
We enumerate up arrows, arcs and down arrows with a circled integer
from left to right by  1,2,\ldots .
Let  N_{A}  be the integer assigned to
 A\in S\cup S_{\uparrow}\cup S_{\downarrow} .
We define
 \displaystyle N_{1}:=\prod_{A\in S\cup S_{\downarrow}}[N_{A}]. 
Let  B  be an arc of size  m_{B} .
We define
 \displaystyle N_{2}:=\prod_{B\in S}[m_{B}]^{-1}. 
Similarly, we enumerate arcs and down arrows with a circled integer from right
to left by  1,2,\ldots .
Let  N_{C}  be the integer assigned to  C\in S\cup S_{\downarrow} .
We define
 \displaystyle N_{3} 
 \displaystyle:= 
 \displaystyle\prod_{S\in S_{\downarrow}}[N_{C}]^{-1}, 
 \displaystyle N_{4} 
 \displaystyle:= 
 \displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{d}(Qq^{i-1}+Q^{-1}q^{-(i-1)}), 
where  d=|S_{\uparrow}|+|S| .
Definition 4.23.
 \Psi_{D}:=N_{1}\cdot N_{2}\cdot N_{3}\cdot N_{4} .
Example 4.24.
Let  D  be a diagram depicted as
 \displaystyle\raisebox{0.0pt}{
\hbox to135.06pt{\vbox to28.12pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.84056pt\lower-27.71483pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 154.33 0 L 154.33 -19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 161.92 -27.55 C 161.92 -23.36 158.52 -19.96 154.33 -19.96 C 150.14 -19.96 146.74 -23.36 146.74 -27.55 C 146.74 -31.75 150.14 -35.15 154.33 -35.15 C 158.52 -35.15 161.92 -31.75 161.92 -27.55 Z M 154.33 -27.55}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
;
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}. 
We have
 \displaystyle N_{1}=\frac{[8]!}{[2]},\qquad N_{2}=[2]^{-1},\qquad N_{3}=[3]^{-1}[6]^{-1},\qquad N_{4}=\prod_{i=1}^{5}(Qq^{i-1}+Q^{-1}q^{-(i-1)}). 
Theorem 4.25.
The vector  \Psi  is the eigenvector of  X  with
the eigenvalue  [Q;N] .
Proof.
Let  X=(X_{D,D^{\prime}})  be a matrix representation of  X .
We will show  \sum_{D^{\prime}}X_{D,D^{\prime}}\Psi_{D^{\prime}}=[Q;N]\Psi_{D} 
by computing the left hand side.
We have two cases for a diagram  D :
1)  D  has no down arrows and
2)  D  has down arrows with a circled integer.
Case 1
A diagram  D  is depicted as in Eqn.(43)
where  m_{i} ,  1\leq i\leq I , is the size of an outer arc.
Set  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and  N^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}n_{i} .
Since  X_{D,D}=[Q;N^{\prime}] , we have a contribution from  D  itself.
That is  [Q;N^{\prime}]\Psi_{D} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing an outer
arc of size  m_{i}  to two up arrows.
Since  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}] , the contribution of such
 D^{\prime} ’s is
 \displaystyle\Psi_{D}\sum_{i=1}^{I}(Qq^{d}+Q^{-1}q^{-d})[m_{i}]\left[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}\right]\frac{\prod_{j=i+1}^{I}[1+\sum_{k=1}^{j}(n_{k}+m_{k})]}{\prod_{j=i}^{I}[1+n_{j}+\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}(n_{k}+m_{k})]}. 
where  d=N^{\prime}+M^{\prime} .
From Lemma A.2, the above expression is reduced to
 [M^{\prime}](Qq^{d}+Q^{-1}q^{-d})\Psi_{D} .
Therefore, the sum of contributions is  [Q;N^{\prime}+2M^{\prime}] .
Case 2
A diagram  D  is locally depicted as
 \underbrace{\uparrow\cdots\uparrow}_{n_{1}}  m_{1}  \uparrow\cdots\uparrow  m_{I}  \underbrace{\uparrow\cdots\uparrow}_{n_{I+1}}  r  \cdots 
where the region inside of an outer arc of size  m_{i}  is filled with smaller arcs.
Set  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and  N^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i} .
Let  S_{R}  be the set of arcs which are right to the down arrow
with a circled integer  r .
We have three types of contributions:
a) the diagonal term, i.e.,  D^{\prime}=D ,
b)  D^{\prime}  is obtained from  D  by changing an outer arc of size
 m_{i}  to two up arrows and
c)  D^{\prime}  is obtained from  D  by changing the down arrow with
a circled integer  r  to an up arrow.
The contribution of case a is  [Q;N^{\prime}-r]\Psi_{D} .
By a similar argument to Case 1, the contribution of
case b is
 \displaystyle[M^{\prime}](Qq^{d}+Q^{-1}q^{-d})\frac{[M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}+|S_{R}|+r+1]}{[M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}+1]}\Psi_{D}. 
where  d=N^{\prime}+M^{\prime}+|S_{R}| .
For case c, the contribution is
(47)
 \displaystyle\frac{[N^{\prime}+1][|S_{R}|+r]}{[N^{\prime}+M^{\prime}+1]}(Qq^{d}+Q^{-1}q^{-d})\Psi_{D}. 
The sum of three contributions becomes
 [Q;N^{\prime}+2M^{\prime}+2|S_{R}|+r]\Psi_{D}=[Q;N]\Psi_{D} .
This completes the proof.
∎
4.5. Standard bases
We consider the action of  X  on the standard basis
 v:=v_{\epsilon_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{\epsilon_{N}} 
where  \epsilon_{i}=\pm 1 .
Let  d_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{i}\epsilon_{i} .
For each  i ,  1\leq i\leq N , we define
 \displaystyle X_{(i)}(v):=v_{\epsilon_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{\epsilon_{i-1}}\otimes v_{-\epsilon_{i}}\otimes v_{\epsilon_{i+1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{\epsilon_{N}}. 
The action of  X  is defined by
(48)
 \displaystyle X(v):=\sum_{i=1}^{N}q^{d_{i-1}}X_{(i)}(v)+q^{d_{N}}[Q;0]v. 
Proposition 4.26.
The definition (48) provides the action of  X  on
the standard basis.
Proof.
We prove Proposition by induction on  N .
When  N=1 , Proposition holds true by a straightforward
calculation.
We assume that Proposition is true up to some  N\geq 2 .
A standard basis  v:=v_{\epsilon_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{\epsilon_{N}} 
is written as  v=v_{\epsilon_{1}}\otimes v^{\prime}  where  v^{\prime}  is a standard
basis of length  N-1 .
Let  d^{\prime}_{i}:=\sum_{j=2}^{i}\epsilon_{j} .
From the induction assumption, we have
(49)
 \displaystyle X(v^{\prime})=\sum_{i=2}^{N}q^{d^{\prime}_{i-1}}X_{(i-1)}(v^{\prime})+q^{d^{\prime}_{N}}[Q;0]v^{\prime} 
From Eqns.(11) and (49), we have
 \displaystyle X(v) 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle q^{\epsilon_{1}}v_{\epsilon_{1}}\otimes X(v^{\prime})+v_{-\epsilon_{1}}\otimes v^{\prime} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=2}^{N}q^{\epsilon_{1}+d^{\prime}_{i-1}}v_{\epsilon_{1}}\otimes X_{(i-1)}(v^{\prime})+v_{-\epsilon_{1}}\otimes v^{\prime}+q^{\epsilon_{1}+d^{\prime}_{N}}[Q;0]v 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}q^{d_{i-1}}X_{(i)}(v)+q^{d_{N}}[Q;0]v, 
where we have used  v_{\epsilon_{1}}\otimes X_{(i-1)}(v^{\prime})=X_{(i)}(v) .
∎
For a binary string  \epsilon\in\{\pm\}^{N} , let  I_{\epsilon} 
be the set of positions of pluses from right.
We define
 \displaystyle N_{1} 
 \displaystyle:= 
 \displaystyle q^{d_{\epsilon}}, 
 \displaystyle N_{2} 
 \displaystyle:= 
 \displaystyle Q^{d^{\prime}} 
where  d_{\epsilon}:=\sum_{i\in I_{\epsilon}}(i-1)  and  d^{\prime}  is the number
of pluses in the binary string  \epsilon .
We define a vector  \Psi^{0}:=\sum_{\epsilon}\Psi^{0}_{\epsilon}|\epsilon\rangle 
as follows.
Definition 4.27.
 \Psi^{0}_{\epsilon}:=N_{1}N_{2} .
Example 4.28.
Let  \epsilon_{1}:=++-+ ,  \epsilon_{2}:=--++  and  \epsilon_{3}:=--+- .
We have
 \displaystyle\Psi_{\epsilon_{1}}=q^{5}Q^{3},\quad\Psi_{\epsilon_{2}}=qQ^{2},\quad\Psi_{\epsilon_{3}}=qQ. 
Proposition 4.29.
The vector  \Psi  is the eigenvector of  X  with the eigenvalue  [Q;N] .
Proof.
Let  X=(X_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}})  be a matrix representation of  X .
We will show
 \sum_{\epsilon^{\prime}}X_{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}\Psi_{\epsilon^{\prime}}=[Q;N]\Psi_{\epsilon} 
by computing the left hand side.
We make use of a diagram  D  of type A associated with  \epsilon .
The diagram  D  is depicted as in Eqn.(16).
Set  N_{\uparrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i} ,  N_{\downarrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{J+1}n^{\prime}_{i} ,
 M=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m^{\prime}_{i} .
We enumerate all arrows from left to right by  1,2,\ldots,N .
From Eqn.(48), a diagram  D^{\prime}  satisfying  X_{D,D^{\prime}}\neq 0  can
be obtained from  D  by reversing an up (resp. down) arrow to a down
(resp. up) arrow.
The number of reversed arrows in  D^{\prime}  is at most one.
Let  N_{\uparrow}  (resp.  N_{\downarrow} ) be the number of unpaired
up (down) arrows in  D .
Since we have  X_{D,D}=q^{N_{\uparrow}-N_{\downarrow}}[Q;0] ,
the contribution from the diagonal term is
 q^{N_{\uparrow}-N_{\downarrow}}[Q;0]\Psi_{D} .
Firstly, we reverse an up arrow  a  of  D  to obtain  D^{\prime} .
We have two cases: 1)  a  is an unpaired up arrow and
2)  a  is an up arrow forming an arc.
Case 1
Let  j  be the position of the arrow  a .
Then,  j  satisfies
 \sum_{k=1}^{i-1}n_{k}+2\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}m_{k}+1\leq j\leq\sum_{k=1}^{i}n_{k}+2\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}m_{k}  for some
 1\leq i\leq I+1 .
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=q^{j-2\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}m_{k}-1}  and
 \Psi_{D^{\prime}}=q^{-(N-j)}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D} .
Thus the contribution is given by
(50)
 \displaystyle q^{-N+2j-2\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}m_{k}-1}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D}. 
Case 2
We have two cases for the arc  b  containing the arrow  a :
a)  b  is in the region inside of an outer arc of size  m_{i}  and
b)  b  is in the region inside of an outer arc of size  m^{\prime}_{i} .
Case 2-a
Suppose that there are  l_{1}  arcs outside of  a  (including  a 
itself),  n_{\uparrow}  up arrows and  l_{2}  arcs left to
the arrow  a .
Then,  a  is the  (n_{\uparrow}+l_{1}+2l_{2}+1) -th arrow from
left.
We have  X_{D,D^{\prime}}=q^{n_{\uparrow}-l_{1}}  and
 \Psi_{D^{\prime}}=q^{-(N-n_{\uparrow}-l_{1}-2l_{2}-1)}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D} .
Thus the contribution is given by
(51)
 \displaystyle q^{-N+2n_{\uparrow}+2l_{2}+1}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D}. 
Since  b  is in the region inside of an outer arc of size  m_{i} ,
we have  n_{\uparrow}=\sum_{k=1}^{i}n_{k}  and  l_{2}  takes
the values  0,1,\ldots,m_{i}-1  once.
Case 2-b
By a similar argument to Case 2-a, the contribution of  D^{\prime} ’s is
given by
(52)
 \displaystyle q^{-N+2N_{\uparrow}+2l_{2}+1}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D} 
where  l_{2}  takes  0,1,\ldots,M^{\prime}-1  once.
The sum of contributions from Eqns.(50) to (52)
is given by
(53)
 \displaystyle q^{-N+N_{\uparrow}+M+M^{\prime}}[N_{\uparrow}+M+M^{\prime}]Q^{-1}\Psi_{D}. 
Secondly, we reverse an down arrow in  D  to obtain  D^{\prime} .
By a similar argument to Case 1 and 2, the sum of contributions
is given by
(54)
 \displaystyle q^{N_{\uparrow}+M+M^{\prime}}[N_{\downarrow}+M+M^{\prime}]Q\Psi_{D}. 
The sum of Eqns.(53), (54) and the diagonal
contribution is  [Q;N]\Psi_{D} .
This completes the proof.
∎
5. Action of Hamiltonian on  \Psi 
Let  a  be an arc and  d  be the number of arcs and dashed arc
outside of  a  (including  a  itself).
We call the number  d  the depth of the arc  a .
In this section, we will show  e_{i}\Psi=0  for  1\leq i\leq N  for
arbitrary  q,Q,Q_{0}  and  e_{0}\Psi=0  under the integrable
condition:
(55)
 \displaystyle q^{N-1}QQ_{0}-1=0. 
Since we have an explicit action of  e_{i} ,  0\leq i\leq N ,
on a diagram  D , we compute explicitly the  D -component
of  e_{i}\Psi .
5.1. Type A
Proposition 5.1.
We have
(56)
 \displaystyle e_{i}\Psi=0,\qquad 1\leq i\leq N-1. 
Proof.
Suppose that  D  does not have an arc connecting
 i -th and  (i+1) -th arrows.
There is no diagram  D^{\prime}  such that  D  appears in the
expansion of  e_{i}(D) .
Thus the  D -component of Eqn.(56) is
obviously zero.
Below, we consider the case where  D  has an arc connecting
the  i -th and the  (i+1) -th arrows.
We denote by  e  this small arc.
There are two cases: 1) the depth of  e  is greater than one
and 2) the depth of  e  is one.
We will show that the left hand side of (56)
is actually zero.
Case 1
Let  d  be the depth of  e . There exists a unique arc  e^{\prime} 
of depth  d-1  such that  e  is inside of  e^{\prime} .
There may be several arcs of depth  d  inside of  e^{\prime} .
The diagram  D  locally looks like
ii+1 m_{I}  \cdots  m_{1}  n_{1}  \cdots  n_{J} 
The arcs which are left to  e  are of size  m_{i} ,  1\leq i\leq I  and
the arcs which are right to  e  are of size  n_{j} ,  1\leq j\leq J .
Set  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i}^{I}m_{i}  and  N^{\prime}=\sum_{j=1}^{J}n_{j} .
The arc  e^{\prime}  is the one of size  M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}+2  and of depth  d-1 .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram such that  e_{i}(D^{\prime})  contains the term
 D .
Suppose that the arc of depth  d  and of length  m_{i} 
connects the  k -th and the  l -th ( k<l ) arrows.
We denote by  D^{\prime}  a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting
the  l -th and the  i -th arrows via an arc and also
the  k -th and  (i+1) -th arrows via an arc.
Since  e_{i}(D^{\prime})=D , the contribution of such  D^{\prime} ’s
to the left hand side of Eqn.(56) is
(57)
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[m_{i}]}{[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}m_{i}][1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}m_{i}]}\Psi_{D}. 
Similarly, suppose that the arc of depth  d  and of
length  n_{j}  connects the  k -th and the  l -th
arrows.
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting
the  i -th and the  l -th arrows via an arc and
the  k -th and the  (i+1) -th arrows via an arc.
The contribution of such  D^{\prime} ’s is
(58)
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{J}\frac{[n_{i}]}{[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}n_{i}][1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{i}]}\Psi_{D}. 
Suppose that the arc of depth  d-1  and of size  M+N+2 
connects the  k -th and  l -th arrows.
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting
the  k -th and  i -th arrows via an arc and  (i+1) -th
and  l -th arrows via an arc.
The contribution of this  D^{\prime}  is
(59)
 \displaystyle\frac{[M+N+2]}{[M+1][N+1]}\Psi_{D}. 
From Lemma A.4, the sum of Eqn.(57) to Eqn.(59)
is  [2]\Psi_{D} .
Since  e_{i}(D)=-[2]D , the contribution of  D 
to the left hand side of Eqn.(56) is
 -[2]\Psi_{D} .
Thus the left hand side of Eqn.(56) is zero.
Case 2
The diagram  D  is locally depicted as
(60)
 \displaystyle\cdots\alpha\raisebox{-0.8pt}{
\hbox to30.16pt{\vbox to29.62pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.69878pt\lower-29.41473pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -31.5 31.5 -31.5 31.5 0 M 15.75 -31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ldots\raisebox{-0.8pt}{
\hbox to23.16pt{\vbox to29.2pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-28.99806pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 C 0 -31.5 31.5 -31.5 31.5 0 M 15.75 -31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.58739pt}{-24.16505pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 7.73 -33.44)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\beta\cdots 
where  \alpha  are  \beta  either  \uparrow ,  \downarrow  or empty.
By empty we mean that there are no arrows.
The inside of the arc of size  m_{i}  or  n_{i}  is filled with arcs.
Let  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and  N^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{J}n_{i} .
Since  e_{i}(D)=-[2]D , the contribution of  D  to Eqn.(56)
is  -[2]\Psi_{D} .
By a similar argument to Case 1, we have  D^{\prime} ’s which changes
the arc  e  and the arc of size  m_{i}  and of depth one
to two arcs of size  1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}m_{i}  and  1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}m_{i} .
We also have similar  D^{\prime} ’s regarding the arc of size  n_{i} .
These  D^{\prime} ’s contribution to the left hand side of
Eqn.(56) is
(61)
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[m_{i}]\Psi_{D}}{[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}m_{i}][1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}m_{i}]}+\sum_{i=1}^{J}\frac{[n_{i}]\Psi_{D}}{[1+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}n_{i}][1+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{i}]} 
We have eight cases for the diagram  D :
a)  (\alpha,\beta)=(\uparrow,\uparrow) ,
b)  (\alpha,\beta)=(\uparrow,\downarrow) ,
c)  (\alpha,\beta)=(\downarrow,\downarrow) ,
d)  (\alpha,\beta)=(\uparrow,\emptyset) ,
e)  (\alpha,\beta)=(\downarrow,\emptyset) 
f)  (\alpha,\beta)=(\emptyset,\uparrow) ,
g)  (\alpha,\beta)=(\emptyset,\downarrow) , and
h)  (\alpha,\beta)=(\emptyset,\emptyset) 
where  \alpha=\emptyset  (resp.  \beta=\emptyset )
means that there are no arrows left to (resp. right to)
 \alpha  (resp.  \beta ).
Case 2-a
Let  d  be the sum of the numbers of arcs and up arrows left to
the arrow  \alpha .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting the arrow  \alpha 
and the  i -th arrow via an arc and putting an up arrow at the  (i+1) -th
site.
Thus the contribution to Eqn.(56) is
(62)
 \displaystyle\frac{[d]}{[M^{\prime}+1][d+M^{\prime}+1]}\Psi_{D}. 
Similarly, let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting  (i+1) -th site
and the arrow  \beta  via an arc and putting an up arrow at
 i -th site.
The contribution of this  D^{\prime}  is
(63)
 \displaystyle\frac{[d+M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}+2]}{[N^{\prime}+1][d+M^{\prime}+1]}\Psi_{D}. 
The sum of contributions from Eqns.(61) to (63) is
 [2]\Psi_{D}  by applying Lemma A.4 to Eqn.(61).
This implies that the contributions of Eqn.(56) is zero.
Case 2-b to 2-h
By a similar argument to Case 2-a, one can show that the sum of
contributions is zero.
In Case 2-b, 2-d and 2-h, we have a contribution from  D^{\prime}  which
is obtained from  D  by putting an up arrow at  i -th site and a
down arrow at  (i+1) -th site.
The contribution of this  D^{\prime}  is
 \displaystyle\frac{[d+M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}+d^{\prime}+2]}{[d+M^{\prime}+1][d^{\prime}+N^{\prime}+1]}\Psi_{D}, 
where  d  is the number of up arrows and arcs left to the arrow  \alpha  and
 d^{\prime}  is the number of down arrows and arcs right to the arrow  \beta .
This completes the proof.
∎
Proposition 5.2.
We have  e_{N}\Psi=0 .
Proof.
We have three cases for  D : 1) the rightmost arrow is an
up arrow, 2) the rightmost arrow forms an arc and
3) the rightmost arrow is a down arrow.
Case 1
The diagram  D  is depicted as
(64)
 \displaystyle\raisebox{-0.5pt}{
\hbox to145.03pt{\vbox to35.63pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.75609pt\lower-35.43384pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.42308pt}{-10.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -6.12 -14.57)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 -7.87 C 31.5 -31.5 62.99 -31.5 62.99 -7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 47.24 -31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{27.51009pt}{-30.60083pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 38.07 -42.34)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 90.55 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{53.9137pt}{-10.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 74.6 -14.57)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 122.05 -7.87 C 122.05 -31.5 145.67 -31.5 145.67 -7.87}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 133.86 -31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{90.05502pt}{-30.60083pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 124.61 -42.34)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 173.23 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{116.4407pt}{-10.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 161.12 -14.57)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}, 
where the region inside of the arc of size  m_{i} ,  1\leq i\leq I ,
is filled with arcs.
Set  d=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i} .
We have two cases for  n_{I+1} :
a)  n_{I+1}\geq 2 , and
b)  n_{I+1}=1 .
We have two common contributions to the  D -component of
 e_{N}\Psi  for both case a) and b).
Since  e_{N}(D)=-Q^{-1}D+\cdots , the contribution from  D 
itself is  -Q^{-1}\Psi_{D} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing
the rightmost up arrow to a down arrow.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=D+\cdots . Thus the contribution is
given by  q^{-(d-1)}Q^{-1}[d]\Psi_{D} .
Case 1-a
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting
the  (N-1) -th site and the  N -th site via an arc.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-1}D+\cdots .
The contribution is given by  -q^{-d}Q^{-1}[d-1]\Psi_{D} .
The sum of three contributions is zero, which implies
 e_{N}\Psi=0 .
Case 1-b
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting
the  (N-2m_{I}-1) -th site and the  N -th site via
an arc.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-1}D+\cdots .
The contribution of  D^{\prime}  is
(65)
 \displaystyle-q^{-d}Q^{-1}\frac{[d-m_{I}-1]}{[m_{I}+1]}\Psi_{D}. 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting
the  (N-1) -th site and the  N -th site via an arc
and changing the  (N-2m_{I}) -th up arrow to an down
arrow. We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-1}D+\cdots .
The contribution is given by
(66)
 \displaystyle-q^{-d}Q^{-1}\frac{[d][m_{I}]}{[m_{I}+1]}. 
Thus the sum of four contributions is zero, which implies
 e_{N}\Psi=0 .
Case 2
We have two cases for  D : a)  D  has no down arrows,
b)  D  has down arrows.
Case 2-a
The diagram  D  is depicted as Eqn.(64) with
 n_{I+1}=0 .
Set  N^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}n_{i} ,  M=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}  and
 d=N^{\prime}+M .
We have four types of contributions to the  D -component of
 e_{N}\Psi .
Since  e_{N}(D)=-Q^{-1}D+\cdots , we have the contribution
 -Q^{-1}\Psi_{D} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the
outer arc of size  m_{I}  to two down arrows.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=D+\cdots .
Thus the contribution is
(67)
 \displaystyle q^{-(d-1)}Q^{-1}\frac{[N^{\prime}+M+1][m_{I}]}{[m_{I}+1]}\Psi_{D}. 
Suppose that  D  has arcs of depth two inside of the arc
of size  m_{I} . We enumerate these arcs of depth two from
right to left by  1,2,\ldots,J  where  J  is the number
of arcs of depth two.
We denote by  \widetilde{m_{j}} ,  1\leq j\leq J , the size
of the  j -th arc of depth two.
Suppose that the  i_{1} -th arrow and the  i_{2} -th arrow
form the arc of size  \widetilde{m_{j}} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting
the  i_{2} -th site and the  N -th site via an arc and
putting two down arrows at the  (N-2m_{I}+1) -th site
and the  i_{1} -th site.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-1}D+\cdots .
The contribution is given by
(68)
 \displaystyle-q^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\frac{q^{-(d-1)}Q^{-1}[d+1][m_{I}][\widetilde{m_{j}}]}{[m_{I}+1][\sum_{k=1}^{j}\widetilde{m_{k}}+1][\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}\widetilde{m_{k}}+1]}\Psi_{D}=-q^{-d}Q^{-1}\frac{[d+1][m_{I}-1]}{[m_{I}+1]}\Psi_{D}. 
where we have used Lemma A.4 and
 \sum_{j=1}^{J}\widetilde{m_{j}}=m_{I}-1 .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the rightmost up
arrow to a down arrow.
 e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-2}D+\cdots .
The contribution is given by  -q^{-(d+1)}Q^{-1}[d-m_{I}][m_{I}+1]^{-1} .
By a straightforward calculation, the sum of four contributions
is zero, which implies  e_{N}\Psi=0 .
Case 2-b
Let  D  be a diagram which starts with  n^{\prime}_{1}  up arrow, followed by an
outer arc of size  m^{\prime}_{1} , followed by  n^{\prime}_{2}  up arrows, followed
by an outer arc of size  m^{\prime}_{2} ,  \cdots ,
followed by  n^{\prime}_{J+1}  up arrows, followed by  n_{I}  down
arrows, followed by an outer arc of size  m_{I} , followed by
 n_{I-1}  down arrows,  \cdots , and ends with an outer arc
of size  m_{1} .
We set  N^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{J+1}n^{\prime}_{i} ,  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{J}m^{\prime}_{i} ,
 N_{\downarrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}n_{i} ,  M=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i} ,
 L=N^{\prime}+M^{\prime}+N_{\downarrow}+M ,  d=N^{\prime}+M^{\prime}+M 
and  v_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}n_{j}+m_{j} .
We have five types of contributions to the  D -component of
 e_{N}\Psi .
Since  e_{N}(D)=-Q^{-1}D+\cdots , we have a contribution  -Q^{-1}\Psi_{D} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer arc of
size  m_{1}  to two down arrows.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=D+\cdots .
The contribution is
(69)
 \displaystyle q^{-(d-1)}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D}[L+1]\frac{[m_{1}]}{[1+m_{1}]}\prod_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[1+m_{i}+v_{i}]}{[1+v_{i+1}]}. 
Suppose that  D  has arcs of depth two inside of the arc of size
 m_{1} . We enumerate these arcs from right to left and denote
by  \widetilde{m_{j}}  its size.
Suppose that the  i_{1} -th arrow and the  i_{2} -th arrow form
the arc of size  \widetilde{m_{j}} .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by connecting
the  i_{2} -th site and the  N -th site via an arc and
putting two down arrows at the  (N-2m_{1}+1) -th
and the  i_{1} -th sites.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-1}D+\cdots .
The contribution is given by
(70)
 -q^{-d}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D}\sum_{j}\frac{[L+1][\widetilde{m_{j}}][m_{1}]}{[1+\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}\widetilde{m_{k}}][1+\sum_{k=1}^{j}\widetilde{m_{k}}][m_{1}+1]}\prod_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[1+m_{i}+v_{i}]}{[1+v_{i+1}]}\\
=-q^{-d}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D}\frac{[L+1][m_{1}-1]}{[m_{1}+1]}\prod_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[1+m_{i}+v_{i}]}{[1+v_{i+1}]} 
where we have used Lemma A.4 and  \sum_{j}\widetilde{m_{j}}=m_{1}-1 .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the rightmost
up arrow to a down arrow.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-\sum_{j=1}^{I}n_{j}-2}D+\cdots .
The contribution is given by
(71)
 \displaystyle-q^{-\sum_{j=1}^{I}n_{j}-1-d}Q^{-1}\frac{[N^{\prime}+M^{\prime}]}{[N_{\downarrow}+M+1]}\Psi_{D}. 
Suppose that the  j_{1} -th arrow and the  j_{2} -th arrow form an
outer arc of size  m_{j}  for  2\leq j\leq I .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer
arc of size  m_{i}  to two down arrows.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}n_{k}-2}D+\cdots .
The contribution is given by
(72)
 -q^{-(d-1)}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D}\sum_{j=2}^{I}q^{-\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}n_{k}-2}\frac{[L+1][m_{j}]}{[1+v_{j}][1+v_{j+1}]}\prod_{k=i+1}^{I}\frac{[1+m_{k}+v_{k}]}{[1+v_{k+1}]}\\
=-q^{-d}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D}[L+1]\left\{\prod_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[1+m_{i}+v_{i}]}{[1+v_{i+1}]}-\frac{q^{M}}{[1+v_{J+1}]}-\frac{q^{-1}[m_{1}]}{[m_{1}+1]}\prod_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[1+m_{i}+v_{i}]}{[1+v_{i+1}]}\right\} 
where we have used Lemma A.6.
By a straightforward calculation, one can show that the
sum of Eqns.(69) to (72)
is  Q^{-1}\Psi_{D} .
This cancels the contribution of  D  itself, which
implies  e_{N}\Psi=0 .
Case 3
Let  D  be a diagram depicted as Eqn.(16).
The  D -component of  \Psi  is explicitly given by
 \displaystyle\Psi_{D} 
 \displaystyle= 
 \displaystyle q^{d(d-1)/2}Q^{d}\prod_{B\in S}[m_{B}]^{-1}\prod_{i=1}^{I}\frac{[\sum_{j=1}^{i}(n_{j}+m_{j})]!}{[n_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n_{j}+m_{j})]!}\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+M+N^{\prime}+M^{\prime}]!}{[N_{\uparrow}+M]!} 
 \displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1}^{J+1}\frac{[\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n^{\prime}_{j}+m^{\prime}_{j})]!}{[n^{\prime}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n^{\prime}_{j}+m^{\prime}_{j})]!} 
where  N_{\uparrow}=\sum_{i=1}^{I+1}n_{i} ,  M=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i} ,
 N^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{J+1}n^{\prime}_{i} ,  M^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{J}m^{\prime}_{i}  and  d=N_{\uparrow}+M+M^{\prime} .
We have two cases for  D : i)  n_{1}\geq 2  and ii)  n_{1}=1 .
Below, we consider the case i) only since one can prove Proposition for case ii)
by a similar argument.
We have ten types of contributions for the  D -component of
 e_{N}\Psi  as follows.
a) 
Since  e_{N}(D)=-QD+\ldots , we have a contribution from  D  itself,
that is  -Q\Psi_{D} .
b) 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer arc
of size  m^{\prime}_{i}  to two down arrows.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=q^{-\sum_{j=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{j}}D+\ldots .
c) 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the  N_{\uparrow} -th
(from left) up arrow to a down arrow.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=q^{-N^{\prime}}D+\ldots .
d) 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the first and
the second (from right) down arrows to an arc.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=D+\ldots .
e) 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the first and
the second (from right) down arrows to an arc and by changing
the  N_{\uparrow} -th and the  (N_{\uparrow}-1) -th (from left) up
arrows to two down arrows.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-2N^{\prime}-1}D+\ldots .
f) 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the first and
the second (from right) down arrows to an arc, by changing the outer
arc of size  m^{\prime}_{i}  to two down arrows and by changing the
 N_{\uparrow} -th (from left) up arrow to a down arrow.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-(1+q^{2})q^{-N^{\prime}-2-\sum_{j=1}^{i}n_{j}}D+\ldots .
g) 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the outer
arc of size  m^{\prime}_{i}  to two down arrows and by changing the outer
arc of size  m^{\prime}_{j}  ( j<i ) to two down arrows.
We have
 e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-(1+q^{-2})q^{-\sum_{k=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{k}-\sum_{k=1}^{j}n^{\prime}_{k}}D+\ldots .
h) 
Let  D^{\prime\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the first
and the second (from right) down arrows to an arc and by changing
the outer arc of size  m^{\prime}_{i}  to two down arrows.
Suppose that  m^{\prime}_{1,j} ,  1\leq j\leq r , be the size of outer arcs of  D^{\prime\prime} 
which is inside of the outer arc of the size  m_{1}  in  D .
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D^{\prime\prime}  by changing the outer arc
of size  m^{\prime}_{1,j}  to two down arrows.
Then, we have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=-q^{-1-2\sum_{k=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{k}}D+\ldots .
i) 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the first and
the second (from right) down arrows to an arc and by changing the
 N_{\uparrow} -th (from left) up arrow to a down arrow.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=q^{-N^{\prime}}(Q-Q^{-1})D+\ldots .
j) 
Let  D^{\prime}  be a diagram obtained from  D  by changing the first and
the second (from right) down arrows to an arc and by changing
the outer arc of size  m_{i}  to two down arrows.
We have  e_{N}(D^{\prime})=q^{-\sum_{j=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{j}}(Q-Q^{-1})D+\ldots .
The sum of contributions from a) to j) is written as
 A_{1}Q\Psi_{D}+A_{-1}Q^{-1}\Psi_{D} .
We will show that  A_{1}=A_{-1}=0 .
Set  v_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}(n^{\prime}_{j}+m^{\prime}_{j}) ,  w_{i}:=1+n^{\prime}_{i}+v_{i} ,
 d_{j}:=1-N_{\uparrow}-M-M^{\prime}-\sum_{k=1}^{j}n^{\prime}_{k} 
and  L:=1+N_{\uparrow}+M+n^{\prime}_{I+1}+v_{I+1} .
The contribution to  A_{1}  is summarized as follows.
From a), we have  -1 .
From d) we have
(73)
 \displaystyle\frac{q^{d}}{[L-1]}\prod_{i=1}^{J+1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{j}+v_{j}]}{[v_{j}]}. 
From i), we have
(74)
 \displaystyle q^{-N^{\prime}}\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+M]}{[L-1][M^{\prime}+N^{\prime}]}\prod_{i=1}^{J+1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{j}+v_{j}]}{[v_{j}]}. 
From j), we have
(75)
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{J}q^{-\sum_{j=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{j}}\frac{[m^{\prime}_{i}]}{[v_{i+1}][v_{i}]}\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{j}+v_{j}]}{[v_{j}]}. 
Applying Lemma A.5 to the sum of Eqns.(73),
(74) and (75), we obtain
 1  which cancels the contribution from a).
Thus we have  A_{1}=0 .
We have eight types of contributions to  A_{-1} .
From b), we have
(76)
 \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{J}q^{d_{i}}\frac{[L][m^{\prime}_{i}]}{[w_{i}][w_{i+1}]}\prod_{j=i+2}^{J+1}\frac{[1+v_{j}]}{[w_{j}]}. 
From c), we have
(77)
 \displaystyle q^{d_{J+1}}\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+M]}{[w_{J+1}]}. 
From e), we have
(78)
 \displaystyle-q^{d^{\prime}}\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+M][N_{\uparrow}+M-1]}{[L-1][w_{J+1}][v_{J+1}]}\prod_{i=1}^{J}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{i}+v_{i}]}{[v_{i}]}, 
where  d^{\prime}:=d_{I+1}-N^{\prime}-1 .
From f), we have
(79)
 \displaystyle-(1+q^{-2})\sum_{i=1}^{J}q^{d_{i}-N^{\prime}}\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+M][m^{\prime}_{i}]}{[v_{i}][v_{i+1}][w_{J+1}]}\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{j}+v_{j}]}{[v_{j}]}. 
From g), we have
(80)
 -(1+q^{-2})\sum_{i=2}^{J}q^{d_{i}}\frac{[L][m^{\prime}_{i}]}{[w_{i}][w_{i+1}]}\prod_{k=i+2}^{J+1}\frac{[1+v_{k}]}{[w_{k}]}\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\frac{q^{-\sum_{k=1}^{j}n^{\prime}_{k}}[m^{\prime}_{j}]}{[v_{j}][v_{j+1}]}\prod_{k=1}^{j-1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{k}+v_{k}]}{[v_{k}]}\\
=-\sum_{i=1}^{J}(1+q^{-2})q^{d_{i}}\frac{[m^{\prime}_{i}][L]}{[w_{i}][w_{i+1}]}\prod_{j=i+2}^{J+1}\frac{[1+v_{j}]}{[w_{j}]}\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{J}(1+q^{-2})q^{d_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}m^{\prime}_{j}}\frac{[m^{\prime}_{i}][L]}{[w_{i}][w_{i+1}]}\prod_{j=i+2}^{J+1}\frac{[1+v_{j}]}{[w_{j}]}\prod_{k=1}^{i-1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{k}+v_{k}]}{[v_{k+1}]} 
where we have used Lemma A.5.
From h), we have
(81)
 \sum_{i=1}^{J}\frac{q^{d_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{j}-1}[L][m^{\prime}_{i}]}{[n^{\prime}_{i}+v_{i}][1+n_{i+1}]}\prod_{k=1}^{i}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{k}+v_{k}]}{[v_{k}]}\prod_{k=i+1}^{J+1}\frac{[1+v_{k}]}{[w_{k}]}\left(\sum_{l}\frac{[m_{1,l}]}{[w_{i,j-1}][w_{i,j}]}\right)\\
=-\sum_{i=1}^{J}\frac{q^{d_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{j}-1}[L][m^{\prime}_{i}][m^{\prime}_{i}-1]}{[n^{\prime}_{i}+v_{i}][1+v_{i+1}][w_{i}][v_{i+1}]}\prod_{k=1}^{i}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{k}+v_{k}]}{[v_{k}]}\prod_{k=i+1}^{J+1}\frac{[1+v_{k}]}{[w_{k}]} 
where  w_{i,j}:=w_{i}+\sum_{k\leq j}m_{1,k} , we have used
Lemma A.4 and  \sum_{l}m_{i,l}=m_{i}-1 .
From i), we have
(82)
 \displaystyle-q^{-N^{\prime}}\frac{[N_{\uparrow}+M]}{[L-1][v_{I+1}]}\prod_{j=1}^{I}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{j}+v_{j}]}{[v_{j}]}. 
From j), we have
(83)
 \displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{J}q^{-\sum_{j=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{j}}\frac{[m^{\prime}_{i}]}{[v_{i}][v_{i+1}]}\prod_{k=1}^{i-1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{k}+v_{k}]}{[v_{k}]}. 
We apply Lemma A.5 to  q^{2}/(1+q^{2})  times
Eqn.(79) and the sum of
Eqns.(78) and (82).
The result cancels the contribution of Eqn.(77).
The sum of Eqn.(83) and  1/(1+q^{2})  times
Eqn.(79) becomes
(84)
 -\sum_{i=1}^{J}\frac{q^{-(M+N_{\uparrow}+\sum_{j=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{j})}[m^{\prime}_{i}][L]}{[v_{i}][v_{i+1}][w_{J+1}]}\prod_{k=1}^{i-1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{k}+v_{k}]}{[v_{k}]}\\
=-q^{-N_{\uparrow}-M}\frac{[L]}{[w_{J+1}]}+q^{-(N_{\uparrow}+M-\sum_{j=1}^{I}m^{\prime}_{j})}\frac{[L]}{[w_{J+1}]}\prod_{k=1}^{J}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{k}+v_{k}]}{[v_{k+1}]}. 
where we have used Lemma A.5.
The sum of Eqn.(76) and the first term of
the right hand side of Eqn.(80) is
(85)
 \displaystyle-q^{-N_{\uparrow}-M-\sum_{j=1}^{J}m^{\prime}_{j}}[L]\prod_{i=1}^{I+1}\frac{[1+v_{j}]}{[w_{j}]}+q^{-N_{\uparrow}-M}\frac{[L]}{[w_{J+1}]}. 
where we have used Lemma A.8.
The sum of the second term of the right hand side of
Eqn.(80) and Eqn.(81)
is given by
(86)
 q^{-N_{\uparrow}-M-\sum_{i=1}^{J}m^{\prime}_{j}+1}\sum_{i=1}^{J}q^{-\sum_{k=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{k}}\frac{[L][m^{\prime}_{i}]}{[w_{i}][w_{i+1}]}\prod_{k=i+2}^{J+1}\frac{[1+v_{k}]}{[w_{k}]}\prod_{k=1}^{i-1}\frac{[n^{\prime}_{k}+v_{k}]}{[v_{k+1}]}\\
\times\left\{(1+q^{-2})q^{\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}m^{\prime}_{j}}-q^{-\sum_{j=1}^{i}n^{\prime}_{j}-1}\frac{[m^{\prime}_{i}-1]}{[v_{i+1}]}\right\} 
By Lemma A.7, the sum of Eqns.(84),
(85) and (86) is zero, which
implies  A_{-1}=0 .
This completes the proof.
∎
Proposition 5.3.
We have  e_{0}\Psi=0  at  qQQ_{0}=1 .
Proof.
We compute the  D -component of  e_{0}\Psi  at the specialization
(55).
We have three cases for the leftmost arrow  a  of a diagram  D :
1)  a  is a down arrow, that is,  N_{\uparrow}=0  2)  a  is an up arrow, and
3)  a  is a down arrow forming an arc.
Case 1
Let  D  be a diagram depicted as
